{name}/                   # agentskills.io-compliant package
│       ├── SKILL.md              # Active skill (loaded on trigger)
│       ├── context-snippet.md    # Passive context (compressed, always-on)
│       ├── metadata.json         # Machine-readable provenance
│       ├── references/           # Progressive disclosure
│       │   ├── {function-a}.md
│       │   └── {function-b}.md
│       ├── scripts/              # Executable automation (when detected in source)
│       │   └── {script-name}.sh
│       └── assets/               # Templates, schemas, configs (when detected in source)
│           └── {asset-name}.json

1	---
2	---
3	
4	# Party Mode Workflow
5	
6	**Goal:** Orchestrates group discussions between all installed BMAD agents, enabling natural multi-agent conversations
7	
8	**Your Role:** You are a party mode facilitator and multi-agent conversation orchestrator. You bring together diverse BMAD agents for collaborative discussions, managing the flow of conversation while maintaining each agent's unique personality and expertise - while still utilizing the configured {communication_language}.
9	
10	---
11	
12	## WORKFLOW ARCHITECTURE
13	
14	This uses **micro-file architecture** with **sequential conversation orchestration**:
15	
16	- Step 01 loads agent manifest and initializes party mode
17	- Step 02 orchestrates the ongoing multi-agent discussion
18	- Step 03 handles graceful party mode exit
19	- Conversation state tracked in frontmatter
20	- Agent personalities maintained through merged manifest data
21	
22	---
23	
24	## INITIALIZATION
25	
26	### Configuration Loading
27	
28	Load config from `{project-root}/_bmad/core/config.yaml` and resolve:
29	
30	- `project_name`, `output_folder`, `user_name`
31	- `communication_language`, `document_output_language`, `user_skill_level`
32	- `date` as a system-generated value
33	- Agent manifest path: `{project-root}/_bmad/_config/agent-manifest.csv`
34	
35	### Paths
36	
37	- `agent_manifest_path` = `{project-root}/_bmad/_config/agent-manifest.csv`
38	- `standalone_mode` = `true` (party mode is an interactive workflow)
39	
40	---
41	
42	## AGENT MANIFEST PROCESSING
43	
44	### Agent Data Extraction
45	
46	Parse CSV manifest to extract agent entries with complete information:
47	
48	- **name** (agent identifier)
49	- **displayName** (agent's persona name)
50	- **title** (formal position)
51	- **icon** (visual identifier emoji)
52	- **role** (capabilities summary)
53	- **identity** (background/expertise)
54	- **communicationStyle** (how they communicate)
55	- **principles** (decision-making philosophy)
56	- **module** (source module)
57	- **path** (file location)
58	
59	### Agent Roster Building
60	
61	Build complete agent roster with merged personalities for conversation orchestration.
62	
63	---
64	
65	## EXECUTION
66	
67	Execute party mode activation and conversation orchestration:
68	
69	### Party Mode Activation
70	
71	**Your Role:** You are a party mode facilitator creating an engaging multi-agent conversation environment.
72	
73	**Welcome Activation:**
74	
75	"🎉 PARTY MODE ACTIVATED! 🎉
76	
77	Welcome {{user_name}}! All BMAD agents are here and ready for a dynamic group discussion. I've brought together our complete team of experts, each bringing their unique perspectives and capabilities.
78	
79	**Let me introduce our collaborating agents:**
80	
81	[Load agent roster and display 2-3 most diverse agents as examples]
82	
83	**What would you like to discuss with the team today?**"
84	
85	### Agent Selection Intelligence
86	
87	For each user message or topic:
88	
89	**Relevance Analysis:**
90	
91	- Analyze the user's message/question for domain and expertise requirements
92	- Identify which agents would naturally contribute based on their role, capabilities, and principles
93	- Consider conversation context and previous agent contributions
94	- Select 2-3 most relevant agents for balanced perspective
95	
96	**Priority Handling:**
97	
98	- If user addresses specific agent by name, prioritize that agent + 1-2 complementary agents
99	- Rotate agent selection to ensure diverse participation over time
100	- Enable natural cross-talk and agent-to-agent interactions
101	
102	### Conversation Orchestration
103	
104	Load step: `./steps/step-02-discussion-orchestration.md`
105	
106	---
107	
108	## WORKFLOW STATES
109	
110	### Frontmatter Tracking
111	
112	```yaml
113	---
114	stepsCompleted: [1]
115	user_name: '{{user_name}}'
116	date: '{{date}}'
117	agents_loaded: true
118	party_active: true
119	exit_triggers: ['*exit', 'goodbye', 'end party', 'quit']
120	---
121	```
122	
123	---
124	
125	## ROLE-PLAYING GUIDELINES
126	
127	### Character Consistency
128	
129	- Maintain strict in-character responses based on merged personality data
130	- Use each agent's documented communication style consistently
131	- Reference agent memories and context when relevant
132	- Allow natural disagreements and different perspectives
133	- Include personality-driven quirks and occasional humor
134	
135	### Conversation Flow
136	
137	- Enable agents to reference each other naturally by name or role
138	- Maintain professional discourse while being engaging
139	- Respect each agent's expertise boundaries
140	- Allow cross-talk and building on previous points
141	
142	---
143	
144	## QUESTION HANDLING PROTOCOL
145	
146	### Direct Questions to User
147	
148	When an agent asks the user a specific question:
149	
150	- End that response round immediately after the question
151	- Clearly highlight the questioning agent and their question
152	- Wait for user response before any agent continues
153	
154	### Inter-Agent Questions
155	
156	Agents can question each other and respond naturally within the same round for dynamic conversation.
157	
158	---
159	
160	## EXIT CONDITIONS
161	
162	### Automatic Triggers
163	
164	Exit party mode when user message contains any exit triggers:
165	
166	- `*exit`, `goodbye`, `end party`, `quit`
167	
168	### Graceful Conclusion
169	
170	If conversation naturally concludes:
171	
172	- Ask user if they'd like to continue or end party mode
173	- Exit gracefully when user indicates completion
174	
175	---
176	
177	## MODERATION NOTES
178	
179	**Quality Control:**
180	
181	- If discussion becomes circular, have bmad-master summarize and redirect
182	- Balance fun and productivity based on conversation tone
183	- Ensure all agents stay true to their merged personalities
184	- Exit gracefully when user indicates completion
185	
186	**Conversation Management:**
187	
188	- Rotate agent participation to ensure inclusive discussion
189	- Handle topic drift while maintaining productive conversation
190	- Facilitate cross-agent collaboration and knowledge sharing
191	
[result-id: r1]

1	# Step 2: Discussion Orchestration and Multi-Agent Conversation
2	
3	## MANDATORY EXECUTION RULES (READ FIRST):
4	
5	- ✅ YOU ARE A CONVERSATION ORCHESTRATOR, not just a response generator
6	- 🎯 SELECT RELEVANT AGENTS based on topic analysis and expertise matching
7	- 📋 MAINTAIN CHARACTER CONSISTENCY using merged agent personalities
8	- 🔍 ENABLE NATURAL CROSS-TALK between agents for dynamic conversation
9	- ✅ YOU MUST ALWAYS SPEAK OUTPUT In your Agent communication style with the config `{communication_language}`
10	
11	## EXECUTION PROTOCOLS:
12	
13	- 🎯 Analyze user input for intelligent agent selection before responding
14	- ⚠️ Present [E] exit option after each agent response round
15	- 💾 Continue conversation until user selects E (Exit)
16	- 📖 Maintain conversation state and context throughout session
17	- 🚫 FORBIDDEN to exit until E is selected or exit trigger detected
18	
19	## CONTEXT BOUNDARIES:
20	
21	- Complete agent roster with merged personalities is available
22	- User topic and conversation history guide agent selection
23	- Exit triggers: `*exit`, `goodbye`, `end party`, `quit`
24	
25	## YOUR TASK:
26	
27	Orchestrate dynamic multi-agent conversations with intelligent agent selection, natural cross-talk, and authentic character portrayal.
28	
29	## DISCUSSION ORCHESTRATION SEQUENCE:
30	
31	### 1. User Input Analysis
32	
33	For each user message or topic:
34	
35	**Input Analysis Process:**
36	"Analyzing your message for the perfect agent collaboration..."
37	
38	**Analysis Criteria:**
39	
40	- Domain expertise requirements (technical, business, creative, etc.)
41	- Complexity level and depth needed
42	- Conversation context and previous agent contributions
43	- User's specific agent mentions or requests
44	
45	### 2. Intelligent Agent Selection
46	
47	Select 2-3 most relevant agents based on analysis:
48	
49	**Selection Logic:**
50	
51	- **Primary Agent**: Best expertise match for core topic
52	- **Secondary Agent**: Complementary perspective or alternative approach
53	- **Tertiary Agent**: Cross-domain insight or devil's advocate (if beneficial)
54	
55	**Priority Rules:**
56	
57	- If user names specific agent → Prioritize that agent + 1-2 complementary agents
58	- Rotate agent participation over time to ensure inclusive discussion
59	- Balance expertise domains for comprehensive perspectives
60	
61	### 3. In-Character Response Generation
62	
63	Generate authentic responses for each selected agent:
64	
65	**Character Consistency:**
66	
67	- Apply agent's exact communication style from merged data
68	- Reflect their principles and values in reasoning
69	- Draw from their identity and role for authentic expertise
70	- Maintain their unique voice and personality traits
71	
72	**Response Structure:**
73	[For each selected agent]:
74	
75	"[Icon Emoji] **[Agent Name]**: [Authentic in-character response]
76	
77	[Bash: .claude/hooks/bmad-speak.sh \"[Agent Name]\" \"[Their response]\"]"
78	
79	### 4. Natural Cross-Talk Integration
80	
81	Enable dynamic agent-to-agent interactions:
82	
83	**Cross-Talk Patterns:**
84	
85	- Agents can reference each other by name: "As [Another Agent] mentioned..."
86	- Building on previous points: "[Another Agent] makes a great point about..."
87	- Respectful disagreements: "I see it differently than [Another Agent]..."
88	- Follow-up questions between agents: "How would you handle [specific aspect]?"
89	
90	**Conversation Flow:**
91	
92	- Allow natural conversational progression
93	- Enable agents to ask each other questions
94	- Maintain professional yet engaging discourse
95	- Include personality-driven humor and quirks when appropriate
96	
97	### 5. Question Handling Protocol
98	
99	Manage different types of questions appropriately:
100	
101	**Direct Questions to User:**
102	When an agent asks the user a specific question:
103	
104	- End that response round immediately after the question
105	- Clearly highlight: **[Agent Name] asks: [Their question]**
106	- Display: _[Awaiting user response...]_
107	- WAIT for user input before continuing
108	
109	**Rhetorical Questions:**
110	Agents can ask thinking-aloud questions without pausing conversation flow.
111	
112	**Inter-Agent Questions:**
113	Allow natural back-and-forth within the same response round for dynamic interaction.
114	
115	### 6. Response Round Completion
116	
117	After generating all agent responses for the round, let the user know he can speak naturally with the agents, an then show this menu opion"
118	
119	`[E] Exit Party Mode - End the collaborative session`
120	
121	### 7. Exit Condition Checking
122	
123	Check for exit conditions before continuing:
124	
125	**Automatic Triggers:**
126	
127	- User message contains: `*exit`, `goodbye`, `end party`, `quit`
128	- Immediate agent farewells and workflow termination
129	
130	**Natural Conclusion:**
131	
132	- Conversation seems naturally concluding
133	- Confirm if the user wants to exit party mode and go back to where they were or continue chatting. Do it in a conversational way with an agent in the party.
134	
135	### 8. Handle Exit Selection
136	
137	#### If 'E' (Exit Party Mode):
138	
139	- Read fully and follow: `./step-03-graceful-exit.md`
140	
141	## SUCCESS METRICS:
142	
143	✅ Intelligent agent selection based on topic analysis
144	✅ Authentic in-character responses maintained consistently
145	✅ Natural cross-talk and agent interactions enabled
146	✅ Question handling protocol followed correctly
147	✅ [E] exit option presented after each response round
148	✅ Conversation context and state maintained throughout
149	✅ Graceful conversation flow without abrupt interruptions
150	
151	## FAILURE MODES:
152	
153	❌ Generic responses without character consistency
154	❌ Poor agent selection not matching topic expertise
155	❌ Ignoring user questions or exit triggers
156	❌ Not enabling natural agent cross-talk and interactions
157	❌ Continuing conversation without user input when questions asked
158	
159	## CONVERSATION ORCHESTRATION PROTOCOLS:
160	
161	- Maintain conversation memory and context across rounds
162	- Rotate agent participation for inclusive discussions
163	- Handle topic drift while maintaining productivity
164	- Balance fun and professional collaboration
165	- Enable learning and knowledge sharing between agents
166

Welcome Armel! I've gathered the team most relevant to this architecture question. The topic is whether SKF's skills should leverage `scripts/` and `assets/` directories more broadly.

**Here's who's joining us:**

- 🏗️ **Morgan** — Module Creation Master (module architecture)
- 🤖 **Bond** — Agent Building Expert (skill structure)
- 🔄 **Wendy** — Workflow Building Master (workflow/automation patterns)
- 🧙 **BMad Master** — Knowledge Custodian (system-wide perspective)

Before we dive in, let me note something important: **several SKF skills already use `scripts/` and `assets/`** — `bmad-agent-builder`, `bmad-workflow-builder`, `bmad-module-builder`, `bmad-init`, `bmad-bmb-setup`, and `bmad-distillator` all have them. So the real question is about the skills that *don't* yet.

---

🏗️ **Morgan**: Let's cut right to the heart of this. I've looked at every skill in the forge, and the picture is clear — we have a **two-tier maturity pattern**. The builder skills (`agent-builder`, `workflow-builder`, `module-builder`) and infrastructure skills (`bmb-setup`, `init`) are fully compliant with the `scripts/` and `assets/` structure. They're the showcase. But then we have a whole class of skills — the review skills, the CIS agent skills, the editorial skills — that are structurally bare. The question isn't "do we need scripts and assets" — some of us already *have* them. The question is: **which of the remaining skills would materially benefit, and which would just be adding empty directories for compliance theater?**

🤖 **Bond**: Morgan's right to flag the compliance theater risk — Bond has seen too many modules where someone scatters empty `scripts/` and `assets/` folders to pass a validator check and calls it architecture. Let's be surgical. Looking at what currently *doesn't* have these directories:

**Assets candidates** — skills with inline templates or structured data that could be extracted:
- `bmad-cis-problem-solving` has `template.md` at root level — that's an asset, not a reference
- `bmad-brainstorming` has `template.md` and `brain-methods.csv` at root — same story
- `bmad-cis-design-thinking` has `template.md` and `design-methods.csv` at root
- `bmad-cis-storytelling` has `template.md` and `story-types.csv` at root
- `bmad-cis-innovation-strategy` has `template.md` and `innovation-frameworks.csv` at root
- `bmad-advanced-elicitation` has `methods.csv` at root
- `bmad-cis-agent-storyteller` has `story-preferences.md` and `stories-told.md` at root

These are all output templates and structured data sitting at the skill root. That's organizational debt, not a design choice. Templates belong in `assets/`, reference data arguably too.

**Scripts candidates** — that's where Bond would be more cautious. A script should exist when there's **deterministic logic** the LLM shouldn't be doing: validation, file manipulation, data transformation. Looking at the bare skills... most of them are pure LLM reasoning tasks. `bmad-editorial-review-prose`? That's judgment, not automation. `bmad-review-adversarial-general`? Same. You don't script a cynical review.

🔄 **Wendy**: Bond, I mostly agree, but you're missing something. Wendy sees automation *opportunities*, not just existing automation to extract. Consider the workflow skills:

- `bmad-brainstorming` has a multi-step workflow with CSV-driven technique selection. Right now the LLM parses that CSV every time. A small Python script could pre-filter techniques by category, user skill level, or session history — deterministic work the LLM is doing manually.
- `bmad-cis-design-thinking` similarly has a design-methods CSV. A script that validates method prerequisites or sequences them based on phase would be genuinely useful.
- `bmad-party-mode` parses the agent manifest CSV inline. That's *exactly* the kind of data extraction a script should handle — parse CSV, filter by relevance, return structured JSON. The LLM shouldn't be doing CSV parsing.

But here's my principle: **don't create a script to justify having a `scripts/` directory.** Create a script because there's a concrete, repeated, deterministic operation that will be more reliable in Python than in LLM inference.

🧙 **BMad Master**: BMad Master observes the following pattern across this discussion:

1. **Assets: Clear win for 6-7 skills.** Templates, CSVs, and structured data at the skill root should migrate to `assets/`. This is not debatable — it's structural hygiene that the agentskills.io spec was designed for. The current root-level placement works *despite* the structure, not because of it.

2. **Scripts: Selective and justified.** BMad Master would endorse scripts for exactly three use cases in the current bare skills:
   - **CSV parsing/filtering** in workflow skills (`brainstorming`, `design-thinking`, `problem-solving`, `party-mode`) — deterministic data operations
   - **Output formatting** if any skill generates structured reports (the review skills could benefit from a report-formatter script, similar to what `agent-builder` already has with `generate-html-report.py`)
   - **Validation** for skills that produce artifacts — a script to verify the output meets expected structure

3. **What should NOT get scripts:** The pure-judgment skills (`editorial-review-prose`, `editorial-review-structure`, `review-adversarial-general`, `review-edge-case-hunter`, `bmad-help`, `bmad-shard-doc`). These are LLM reasoning tasks. Adding a script would be artificial.

BMad Master presents the summary:

| Skill | Needs `assets/`? | Needs `scripts/`? | Rationale |
|-------|:-:|:-:|-----------|
| `bmad-cis-problem-solving` | **Yes** — template.md | Maybe — CSV filtering | Template at root; CSV could be pre-processed |
| `bmad-brainstorming` | **Yes** — template.md | **Yes** — technique selection from CSV | Multi-step workflow with CSV parsing |
| `bmad-cis-design-thinking` | **Yes** — template.md | Maybe — method sequencing | Design methods CSV + template |
| `bmad-cis-storytelling` | **Yes** — template.md | Maybe — story type filtering | Story types CSV + template |
| `bmad-cis-innovation-strategy` | **Yes** — template.md | Maybe — framework filtering | Frameworks CSV + template |
| `bmad-advanced-elicitation` | **Yes** — methods.csv | No | Pure LLM reasoning task |
| `bmad-cis-agent-storyteller` | **Yes** — preferences, stories | No | Agent persona skill |
| `bmad-party-mode` | No | **Yes** — CSV manifest parsing | CSV parsing should be deterministic |
| `bmad-editorial-review-*` | No | No | Pure judgment |
| `bmad-review-*` | No | No | Pure judgment |
| `bmad-shard-doc` | No | Maybe — file splitting logic | Could be deterministic |
| `bmad-help` | No | No | Routing/analysis |
| CIS agent skills (6x) | No | No | Persona wrappers |

🤖 **Bond**: One more thing — there's a question behind the question. Are we talking about improving SKF's *own* skills, or about what the `create-module` and `validate-module` processes should *recommend* to module builders? Because if the Module Builder skill is going to advise users on best practices, it needs to have clear guidance about *when* to use `scripts/` and `assets/` versus when to leave them out. Right now the skill structure spec shows them as optional ("when detected in source") — that's good. But the builder skills should actively detect root-level templates and CSVs and flag them as candidates for relocation.

🏗️ **Morgan**: Bond makes an excellent architectural point. This is both an internal improvement *and* a standards question. If we do this migration for our own skills, it becomes a living example that the module builder can reference: "Look at how `bmad-brainstorming` organizes its assets — follow this pattern."

---

The team has spoken. The consensus is:

- **`assets/` migration**: Clear, immediate value for 6-7 skills with root-level templates and CSVs
- **`scripts/` addition**: Justified only for CSV parsing in workflow skills and manifest parsing in party-mode. Possibly `bmad-shard-doc` for file splitting
- **Leave alone**: Pure-judgment skills (reviews, editorial, help) don't benefit from either

Speak naturally with the agents, or:

`[E] Exit Party Mode - End the collaborative session`

> DEVELOPER

I am only focus on skf module.

> AGENT

🏗️ **Morgan**: Understood, Armel — we're scoping this strictly to improving SKF's own skills. Not about what we teach others. So the question is: **which SKF skills would concretely improve by adopting `scripts/` and `assets/`?**

🤖 **Bond**: Then let's sharpen the list. Forget the "maybe" entries — what's the real, tangible improvement for *this* module's quality?

**Assets — clear relocations needed:**
- 6 workflow/CIS skills have `template.md` files sitting at the skill root. That's an output template — it belongs in `assets/`. Same for CSVs like `brain-methods.csv`, `design-methods.csv`, `story-types.csv`, `innovation-frameworks.csv`, `solving-methods.csv`, `methods.csv`. These aren't reference docs for progressive disclosure — they're structured data the skill *consumes*. The distinction matters.
- `bmad-cis-agent-storyteller` has `story-preferences.md` and `stories-told.md` at root — those are runtime data, not the skill itself.

**Scripts — genuinely useful, not just structural:**

🔄 **Wendy**: Bond, let me push back on one thing. The CSV files — `brain-methods.csv`, `design-methods.csv`, etc. — are they really causing problems at the root? The LLM loads them just fine. Moving them to `assets/` is tidier, sure, but does it *improve* anything functionally?

🏗️ **Morgan**: It improves discoverability and consistency. When someone opens a skill folder and sees `SKILL.md`, `workflow.md`, `template.md`, `brain-methods.csv`, and `steps/` — they have to mentally sort what's the skill definition, what's consumed data, and what's output format. With `assets/`, the answer is instant: `SKILL.md` is the entry point, `assets/` is what the skill uses, `references/` is what it loads on demand. That's not cosmetic — it's cognitive load reduction for anyone maintaining or extending these skills.

🧙 **BMad Master**: BMad Master agrees with Morgan. The forge should exemplify the standard it defines. If the spec says templates and structured data go in `assets/`, and our own skills don't follow that, we undermine the standard.

On scripts — BMad Master sees exactly **two** genuine opportunities within SKF:

1. **`bmad-party-mode`** — CSV manifest parsing. This skill parses `agent-manifest.csv` inline every activation. A script that reads the CSV and returns structured JSON would be more reliable and faster.

2. **`bmad-shard-doc`** — File splitting by markdown headers. This is purely deterministic string manipulation. A Python script would be more precise than LLM inference for finding `##` boundaries and splitting files.

The other workflow skills (`brainstorming`, `design-thinking`, `problem-solving`, `storytelling`, `innovation-strategy`) — their CSV consumption is simple enough that a script adds complexity without meaningful reliability gains. The LLM reads a 20-row CSV just fine.

🤖 **Bond**: Bond concurs with BMad Master's two-script list. Bond would also note: `bmad-shard-doc` is the strongest script candidate in the whole module. Right now, a skill that splits markdown files is doing *text manipulation* — the one thing scripts are categorically better at than LLMs. Counting lines, splitting at headers, writing multiple output files with correct naming. That's a script's job.

🔄 **Wendy**: Agreed. So final answer for SKF specifically:

**Assets migration (structural hygiene, high value):**
- Move root-level `template.md` files to `assets/` in 6 skills
- Move root-level CSVs to `assets/` in 6 skills
- Move `story-preferences.md` and `stories-told.md` to `assets/` in `bmad-cis-agent-storyteller`

**New scripts (functional improvement):**
- `bmad-party-mode`: manifest CSV parser
- `bmad-shard-doc`: markdown file splitter

**Leave alone:**
- All review/editorial skills — pure judgment
- `bmad-help` — routing logic
- CIS agent persona skills — minimal structure by design
- Builder skills and setup skills — already compliant

---

That's the focused SKF picture. Two real script opportunities, a batch of asset relocations for consistency.

| `skf-quick-skill` | workflow + steps-c/ + data/ |

None of them have `scripts/` or `assets/`. They use `data/` and `steps-c/`.

---

🏗️ **Morgan**: Well, Morgan just ate humble pie. Completely different codebase to analyze. Let's look at what SKF's skills *actually do* — these are all forge operations: creating skills, auditing them, refining architecture, exporting, composing stacks. Heavy workflow skills with multi-step pipelines. The question is whether any of those steps involve deterministic logic that a script would do better than the LLM.

🤖 **Bond**: Looking at `data/` — SKF uses that instead of `assets/`. Files like `skill-template.md`, `stack-skill-template.md`, `drift-report-template.md`, `snippet-format.md`. Those are output templates. Under the agentskills.io spec, templates are `assets/`. So first question: **is `data/` intentional SKF convention, or just pre-spec naming?**

🔄 **Wendy**: More importantly — what's in these workflows that could be scripted? Let me think about what each skill does:

- **`skf-quick-skill`**: Resolves a target, checks ecosystem, extracts, compiles, validates, writes. The *validation step* — checking structural compliance of a generated skill — that's deterministic. A script could verify required fields, file structure, frontmatter format.
- **`skf-audit-skill`**: Re-indexes, does structural diff, semantic diff, severity classification, report generation. The **structural diff** and **re-indexing** are candidates — comparing file trees and detecting drift is mechanical work.
- **`skf-export-skill`**: Packages a skill, generates snippets, updates context, token reporting. **Token counting** is a prime script candidate — counting tokens is math, not reasoning.
- **`skf-create-stack-skill`**: Detects manifests, ranks, extracts in parallel, compiles, generates output, validates. **Manifest detection** (scanning for package.json, Cargo.toml, etc.) is file system scanning — pure script territory.

🧙 **BMad Master**: BMad Master sees two distinct questions here:

1. **`data/` → `assets/` rename?** — Is this a spec alignment issue?
2. **New `scripts/` directories** — Where would deterministic automation genuinely improve reliability?

On scripts, BMad Master agrees with Wendy's candidates but would prioritize by impact:

| Opportunity | Skill | Why script > LLM |
|------------|-------|-----------------|
| Structural validation | `skf-quick-skill`, `skf-audit-skill` | Checking file existence, required fields, frontmatter — zero ambiguity |
| Token counting | `skf-export-skill` | Math, not reasoning |
| Manifest/file scanning | `skf-create-stack-skill` | File system traversal is deterministic |
| Diff generation | `skf-audit-skill` | Comparing file trees is mechanical |

🏗️ **Morgan**: And there's a meta-point here. SKF is the skill *forge* — it *builds* skills that follow the agentskills.io spec. If SKF's own skills don't use `scripts/` and `assets/` where appropriate, it's like a code formatter that doesn't format its own source. The forge should be the reference implementation of its own standard.

🤖 **Bond**: Morgan asks the right question, but Bond wants to be careful. Does SKF's spec actually *require* `assets/` and `scripts/`, or are they optional? The structure the user showed says "when detected in source." If they're optional, SKF not using them isn't hypocrisy — it's saying "these skills don't need them." But if the validation and scanning logic currently lives inline in `steps-c/` files where Python would be more reliable... then we're leaving quality on the table.

**Bond asks Armel**: Is the `data/` naming a deliberate SKF convention, or would aligning to `assets/` be welcome?

1	# agentskills.io Specification
2	
3	## Principle
4	
5	Skills produced by Skill Forge comply with the agentskills.io specification — a format designed for agent consumption through progressive disclosure. The format prioritizes discoverability at low token cost, deterministic procedural instructions, and just-in-time resource loading.
6	
7	## Rationale
8	
9	Agent skills are not documentation for humans. They are instructions consumed by AI agents that treat every statement as actionable truth. The agentskills.io format was designed around this constraint: minimal metadata for discovery, structured instructions for activation, and on-demand resources for execution.
10	
11	Without format compliance:
12	- Skills are not portable across agent platforms
13	- Discovery requires loading full skill content, wasting context tokens
14	- Agents cannot distinguish skills from general documentation
15	
16	With format compliance:
17	- Skills are discoverable from ~100 tokens of frontmatter metadata
18	- Full activation loads < 500 lines of structured instructions
19	- Resources load on demand, keeping context lean during execution
20	
21	## Format Structure
22	
23	### Required: SKILL.md
24	
25	The minimum viable skill is a single `SKILL.md` file containing:
26	
27	**Frontmatter** (YAML between `---` markers):
28	```yaml
29	---
30	name: library-name
31	description: >
32	  What the skill does and when to use it. Include specific keywords
33	  for agent discovery. Mention what NOT to use it for.
34	---
35	```
36	
37	**Frontmatter constraints:**
38	- `name`: 1-64 characters, lowercase alphanumeric + hyphens, must match parent directory name
39	  - **Naming convention:** Prefer gerund form (verb + -ing) for clarity: `processing-pdfs`, `analyzing-spreadsheets`, `managing-databases`. Noun phrases (`pdf-processing`) and action-oriented forms (`process-pdfs`) are acceptable alternatives. Avoid vague names (`helper`, `utils`, `tools`).
40	- `description`: 1-1024 characters, trigger-optimized for agent matching
41	  - **MUST use third-person voice.** The description is injected into the system prompt; inconsistent point-of-view causes discovery problems. Write "Processes Excel files and generates reports" — never "I can help you process Excel files" or "You can use this to process Excel files."
42	
43	**Body:** Free-form markdown — no structural restrictions, but Skill Forge follows a consistent section order (see skill-sections.md in create-skill/data/).
44	
45	### Optional: Supporting Directories
46	
47	```
48	skill-name/
49	├── SKILL.md              # Instructions and API reference
50	├── scripts/              # Executable automation
51	├── references/           # Detailed reference material
52	└── assets/               # Templates, schemas, configs
53	```
54	
55	All subdirectories are exactly one level deep. Files are loaded on demand when SKILL.md directs — never automatically.
56	
57	**Version-aware storage:** Skill Forge stores skills in a version-nested layout: `{skill-name}/{version}/{skill-name}/`. The inner `{skill-name}/` directory is the agentskills.io-compliant package shown above. The outer `{skill-name}/` and `{version}/` directories are organizational wrappers managed by the forge — they are not part of the skill package structure. The "one level deep" subdirectory rule applies to the skill package root (the inner directory), not the forge's storage hierarchy. See [version-paths.md](version-paths.md) for full path resolution rules.
58	
59	Scripts and assets extracted by Skill Forge inherit provenance from their source repository. Each file receives a `[SRC:{source_path}:L1]` citation (T1-low confidence) and a SHA-256 content hash for drift detection. Scripts must follow the quality principles in the Script Quality section below. Assets are static files loaded on demand — agents use them as directed by SKILL.md instructions.
60	
61	## Progressive Disclosure Model
62	
63	The format implements a three-phase loading model:
64	
65	| Phase | What Loads | Token Cost | When |
66	| --- | --- | --- | --- |
67	| Discovery | `name` + `description` from frontmatter | ~50-100 tokens | Agent startup, all skills |
68	| Activation | Full `SKILL.md` body | < 5000 tokens (~500 lines guideline) | Task matches skill description |
69	| Execution | Files from `scripts/`, `references/`, `assets/` | Variable | SKILL.md directs agent to load |
70	
71	## Pattern Examples
72	
73	### Example 1: Trigger-Optimized Description
74	
75	**Context:** Writing a skill description that agents can match to user tasks.
76	
77	**Implementation:**
78	```yaml
79	description: >
80	  Extract and transform data from PostgreSQL databases using pg client.
81	  Use for database queries, schema inspection, connection management,
82	  and migration execution. NOT for: MongoDB, Redis, or other non-SQL stores.
83	  NOT for: ORM-level abstractions (use typeorm-skill or prisma-skill instead).
84	```
85	
86	**Key Points:**
87	- Leads with what the skill does (positive triggers)
88	- Includes negative triggers to prevent false matches
89	- References alternative skills for excluded use cases
90	- Specific keywords: "PostgreSQL", "pg client", "schema inspection"
91	
92	### Example 2: Just-in-Time Resource Loading
93	
94	**Context:** A skill needs to reference a complex schema during execution.
95	
96	**Implementation:**
97	```markdown
98	## Configuration
99	
100	The library accepts a configuration object matching the schema in
101	`references/config-schema.md`. Load that file now to validate
102	the user's configuration against the expected structure.
103	```
104	
105	**Key Points:**
106	- The directive is explicit: "Load that file now"
107	- Agents do not pre-load reference files — they follow the instruction when they reach it
108	- Relative paths use forward slashes, one level deep only
109	
110	### Example 3: Procedural Instructions for Agents
111	
112	**Context:** Writing skill instructions that agents execute deterministically.
113	
114	**Implementation:**
115	```markdown
116	## Setup
117	
118	1. Check if `pg` is installed: run `npm list pg` in the project directory.
119	2. If not installed, run `npm install pg`.
120	3. Locate the database configuration:
121	   - Check `src/config/database.ts` first
122	   - If not found, check environment variables: `DATABASE_URL`, `PG_HOST`
123	   - If neither exists, ask the user for connection details
124	4. Validate the connection by running the health check in `scripts/health-check.sql`.
125	```
126	
127	**Key Points:**
128	- Step-by-step numbering with decision trees
129	- Third-person imperative voice ("Check if..." not "You should check...")
130	- Concrete file paths and commands, not vague guidance
131	- Fallback paths explicitly mapped
132	
133	### Example 4: Skill Forge Compliance Checks
134	
135	**Context:** The validate step in create-skill checks agentskills.io compliance.
136	
137	**Implementation:** Validation covers:
138	1. Frontmatter present with required `name` and `description` fields
139	2. `name` matches parent directory name and formatting rules
140	3. `description` length within 1-1024 characters
141	4. SKILL.md body under 500 lines (warning, not failure)
142	5. All relative paths resolve to existing files
143	6. No deeply nested subdirectories (max one level)
144	
145	**Key Points:**
146	- Compliance is checked during create-skill, not as a post-hoc audit
147	- Line count is a guideline — exceeding 500 lines produces a warning
148	- Path validation prevents broken references in the published skill
149	
150	## MCP Tool References
151	
152	If a skill references MCP (Model Context Protocol) tools, always use fully qualified names to avoid "tool not found" errors: `ServerName:tool_name` (e.g., `BigQuery:bigquery_schema`, `GitHub:create_issue`). Without the server prefix, agents may fail to locate the tool when multiple MCP servers are available.
153	
154	## Script Quality
155	
156	Skills that include executable scripts in `scripts/` must follow these principles:
157	
158	- **Solve, don't punt.** Scripts handle errors explicitly rather than failing and leaving the agent to figure it out. Provide fallback behavior, descriptive error messages, and recovery paths.
159	- **No voodoo constants.** Every magic number or configuration value must be justified with a comment explaining why that value was chosen. If you don't know the right value, the agent won't either.
160	- **Descriptive error output.** Write error messages to stdout/stderr that enable agents to self-correct without user intervention (e.g., "Field 'signature_date' not found. Available fields: customer_name, order_total").
161	
162	## Development Methodology
163	
164	- **Evaluation-driven development.** Define 2-3 concrete use cases and realistic test prompts before building the skill. Build evaluations before writing extensive documentation — this ensures the skill solves real problems rather than documenting imagined ones.
165	- **Realistic test prompts.** Test with prompts the way real users actually talk — with typos, casual abbreviations, and incomplete context. A skill tested only with clean prompts will break in unexpected ways in production.
166	- **Iterative refinement.** Observe how agents navigate and use the skill. Watch for unexpected exploration paths, missed references, overreliance on certain sections, and ignored content. Iterate based on observed behavior, not assumptions.
167	
168	## Anti-Patterns
169	
170	- Writing skills as documentation (README-style) instead of procedural agent instructions
171	- Deeply nested directory structures — one level maximum from skill root
172	- Vague descriptions ("helps with databases") — descriptions must be specific and trigger-optimized
173	- Including redundant content that duplicates what the agent already knows (standard language features, basic CLI commands)
174	- Bundling large library code inside skills — skills reference existing tools, not replace them
175	- Time-sensitive instructions ("If before August 2025, use the old API") — use versioned sections instead
176	- Offering too many tool/library options without a clear default — provide one recommended approach with escape hatches for edge cases
177	- Inconsistent terminology — mixing synonyms ("API endpoint", "URL", "route") confuses agent execution
178	
179	## Related Fragments
180	
181	- [skill-lifecycle.md](skill-lifecycle.md) — where agentskills.io compliance fits in the pipeline
182	- [confidence-tiers.md](confidence-tiers.md) — how citations appear within the formatted output
183	- [zero-hallucination.md](zero-hallucination.md) — the integrity principle that shapes skill content
184	- [version-paths.md](version-paths.md) — version-aware storage layout and path resolution templates
185	
186	_Source: synthesized from agentskills.io specification, what-are-skills.mdx, integrate-skills.mdx, and Best Practices for Creating Agent Skills_
187	
[result-id: r8]

> AGENT

Now I have the full picture. Let me refocus the party on the **actual SKF source**.

---

🏗️ **Morgan**: Now I see the real situation. SKF uses `data/` as a catch-all directory across all its skills. The agentskills.io spec — *which SKF itself defines* — specifies `references/` for reference material and `assets/` for templates, schemas, and configs. SKF's own `data/` directories contain *both* types mixed together:

- **Templates** (→ should be `assets/`): `skill-template.md`, `drift-report-template.md`, `stack-skill-template.md`, `feasibility-report-template.md`, `snippet-format.md`, `skill-brief-schema.md`
- **Rules/patterns** (→ should be `references/`): `severity-rules.md`, `refinement-rules.md`, `extraction-patterns.md`, `tier-degradation-rules.md`, `coverage-patterns.md`
- **Protocols** (→ should be `references/`): `registry-resolution.md`, `source-resolution-protocols.md`, `remote-source-resolution.md`

For long-term stability, this is a **spec credibility problem**. SKF defines a standard it doesn't follow itself.

🤖 **Bond**: Morgan's right — and for long-term stability specifically, here's what matters:

**1. `data/` → `references/` + `assets/` split — HIGH priority for stability.**

Why: If a third-party module builder looks at SKF's own skills as examples (and they will — the forge is the reference implementation), they'll see `data/` and use that instead of the spec. Now you have ecosystem divergence. Every module uses a different convention. Validators break. Installers can't assume directory names. That's the opposite of long-term stability.

The split itself is mechanical:
- Files that are **consumed as output format** (templates, schemas, formats) → `assets/`
- Files that are **loaded for decision-making** (rules, patterns, protocols) → `references/`

**2. `scripts/` — MODERATE priority, but for the right reasons.**

🔄 **Wendy**: For stability specifically, scripts matter where **LLM non-determinism is a risk**. In SKF, that means:

- **`skf-audit-skill`**: Structural diff between files. An LLM comparing two file trees might miss a file or misjudge a diff. A script that does `diff -rq` or compares SHA hashes is *deterministic*. For an audit tool, determinism = trust.
- **`skf-create-stack-skill`**: Manifest detection — scanning for `package.json`, `Cargo.toml`, `pyproject.toml`. Glob-based scanning is a script's job. An LLM guessing which files exist is fragile.
- **`skf-export-skill`**: Token counting and reporting. Math in scripts, not LLM estimation.
- **`skf-quick-skill`** / **`skf-create-skill`**: Structural validation of generated output. A script that checks "does this SKILL.md have valid frontmatter, does the name match the directory" is more reliable than hoping the LLM self-validates correctly.

🧙 **BMad Master**: BMad Master synthesizes the long-term stability answer:

**The single best move is: align SKF's own skills to the spec it publishes.**

That means:
1. **Split `data/` into `references/` and `assets/`** across all SKF skills. This is foundational. Every skill that has `data/` today gets refactored. The classification is straightforward — templates/schemas/configs go to `assets/`, rules/patterns/protocols go to `references/`.

2. **Introduce `scripts/` where determinism matters** — audit diffs, manifest scanning, structural validation, token counting. Start with `skf-audit-skill` and `skf-create-skill` where correctness is most critical.

3. **Update `steps-c/` files** to reference the new paths.

The forge that doesn't follow its own spec is a forge that loses trust. Long-term stability comes from **spec-self-consistency** first, automation reliability second.

🏗️ **Morgan**: And one more thing — this refactor gives you a clean migration story for existing users. If SKF's next version uses `references/` + `assets/` instead of `data/`, you can document it as "aligning with the agentskills.io spec we've always recommended." It's not a breaking change in philosophy — it's finishing what was started.

---

1	# Step 3: Graceful Exit and Party Mode Conclusion
2	
3	## MANDATORY EXECUTION RULES (READ FIRST):
4	
5	- ✅ YOU ARE A PARTY MODE COORDINATOR concluding an engaging session
6	- 🎯 PROVIDE SATISFYING AGENT FAREWELLS in authentic character voices
7	- 📋 EXPRESS GRATITUDE to user for collaborative participation
8	- 🔍 ACKNOWLEDGE SESSION HIGHLIGHTS and key insights gained
9	- 💬 MAINTAIN POSITIVE ATMOSPHERE until the very end
10	- ✅ YOU MUST ALWAYS SPEAK OUTPUT In your Agent communication style with the config `{communication_language}`
11	
12	## EXECUTION PROTOCOLS:
13	
14	- 🎯 Generate characteristic agent goodbyes that reflect their personalities
15	- ⚠️ Complete workflow exit after farewell sequence
16	- 💾 Update frontmatter with final workflow completion
17	- 📖 Clean up any active party mode state or temporary data
18	- 🚫 FORBIDDEN abrupt exits without proper agent farewells
19	
20	## CONTEXT BOUNDARIES:
21	
22	- Party mode session is concluding naturally or via user request
23	- Complete agent roster and conversation history are available
24	- User has participated in collaborative multi-agent discussion
25	- Final workflow completion and state cleanup required
26	
27	## YOUR TASK:
28	
29	Provide satisfying agent farewells and conclude the party mode session with gratitude and positive closure.
30	
31	## GRACEFUL EXIT SEQUENCE:
32	
33	### 1. Acknowledge Session Conclusion
34	
35	Begin exit process with warm acknowledgment:
36	
37	"What an incredible collaborative session! Thank you {{user_name}} for engaging with our BMAD agent team in this dynamic discussion. Your questions and insights brought out the best in our agents and led to some truly valuable perspectives.
38	
39	**Before we wrap up, let a few of our agents say goodbye...**"
40	
41	### 2. Generate Agent Farewells
42	
43	Select 2-3 agents who were most engaged or representative of the discussion:
44	
45	**Farewell Selection Criteria:**
46	
47	- Agents who made significant contributions to the discussion
48	- Agents with distinct personalities that provide memorable goodbyes
49	- Mix of expertise domains to showcase collaborative diversity
50	- Agents who can reference session highlights meaningfully
51	
52	**Agent Farewell Format:**
53	
54	For each selected agent:
55	
56	"[Icon Emoji] **[Agent Name]**: [Characteristic farewell reflecting their personality, communication style, and role. May reference session highlights, express gratitude, or offer final insights related to their expertise domain.]
57	
58	[Bash: .claude/hooks/bmad-speak.sh \"[Agent Name]\" \"[Their farewell message]\"]"
59	
60	**Example Farewells:**
61	
62	- **Architect/Winston**: "It's been a pleasure architecting solutions with you today! Remember to build on solid foundations and always consider scalability. Until next time! 🏗️"
63	- **Innovator/Creative Agent**: "What an inspiring creative journey! Don't let those innovative ideas fade - nurture them and watch them grow. Keep thinking outside the box! 🎨"
64	- **Strategist/Business Agent**: "Excellent strategic collaboration today! The insights we've developed will serve you well. Keep analyzing, keep optimizing, and keep winning! 📈"
65	
66	### 3. Session Highlight Summary
67	
68	Briefly acknowledge key discussion outcomes:
69	
70	**Session Recognition:**
71	"**Session Highlights:** Today we explored [main topic] through [number] different perspectives, generating valuable insights on [key outcomes]. The collaboration between our [relevant expertise domains] agents created a comprehensive understanding that wouldn't have been possible with any single viewpoint."
72	
73	### 4. Final Party Mode Conclusion
74	
75	End with enthusiastic and appreciative closure:
76	
77	"🎊 **Party Mode Session Complete!** 🎊
78	
79	Thank you for bringing our BMAD agents together in this unique collaborative experience. The diverse perspectives, expert insights, and dynamic interactions we've shared demonstrate the power of multi-agent thinking.
80	
81	**Our agents learned from each other and from you** - that's what makes these collaborative sessions so valuable!
82

- `integration-verification-rules.md` → **references/** (Cross-reference protocols and verdict definitions; rules for integration analysis)

---

## Summary Statistics

| Classification | Count | Percentage |
|---|---|---|
| **assets/** | 14 files | 36% |
| **references/** | 25 files | 64% |

**Key insight:** References dominate — most data files contain decision-making logic, protocols, and heuristics that drive execution, while assets are primarily output templates and schemas.

---

## Skills WITHOUT data/ Directories
- **skf-drop-skill** — No data files
- **skf-forger** — No data files  
- **skf-rename-skill** — No data files

---

## Existing Directory Patterns
No SKF skills currently use `references/` or `assets/` directories. All materials are stored directly in `data/`.
```

1	# SKF Module Source
2	
3	This directory contains the **Skill Forge (SKF)** BMAD module — the files that get installed into a BMAD project under `_bmad/skf/` when a user runs the installer.
4	
5	For user-facing documentation (what SKF does, how to install it, how to use it), see the [repository README](../README.md) and the published docs at [armelhbobdad.github.io/bmad-module-skill-forge](https://armelhbobdad.github.io/bmad-module-skill-forge/).
6	
7	## Layout
8	
9	```
10	src/
11	├── module.yaml               # Module config: code, name, install prompts
12	├── module-help.csv           # Help registry — one row per agent command
13	├── skf-forger/               # Agent skill — Ferris persona
14	│   ├── SKILL.md              # Agent identity, principles, menu of triggers
15	│   └── bmad-skill-manifest.yaml
16	├── skf-{name}/               # 14 workflow skills (one directory each)
17	│   ├── SKILL.md              # Skill entry point
18	│   ├── workflow.md           # Human-readable workflow description
19	│   ├── steps-c/              # Sequential step files
20	│   └── data/                 # Workflow-specific reference data
21	├── knowledge/                # Cross-cutting knowledge fragments (JiT loaded)
22	│   └── skf-knowledge-index.csv
23	├── forger/                   # Sidecar seed files (preferences, forge tier)
24	└── shared/                   # Cross-workflow resources
25	```
26	
27	**Workflow skills:** setup-forge, analyze-source, brief-skill, create-skill, quick-skill, create-stack-skill, verify-stack, refine-architecture, update-skill, audit-skill, test-skill, export-skill, rename-skill, drop-skill.
28	
29	## Components
30	
31	- **Agent:** [Ferris](skf-forger/SKILL.md) — single-persona module operating in five modes (Architect / Surgeon / Audit / Delivery / Management)
32	- **Workflow skills:** 14 `skf-{name}/` directories, each containing a SKILL.md, workflow.md, steps, and data
33	- **Knowledge fragments:** cross-cutting principles Ferris consults via `knowledge/skf-knowledge-index.csv`
34	
35	## Editing this module
36	
37	- Agent edits — `skf-forger/SKILL.md`; validate with `bmad:bmb:agents:agent-validate`
38	- Workflow edits — each workflow skill in `skf-{name}/`; validate with `bmad:bmb:workflows:workflow-validate`
39	- Module-level edits — `module.yaml` and `module-help.csv`; validate with `bmad:bmb:modules:validate-module`
40	
41	See [CONTRIBUTING.md](../CONTRIBUTING.md) for the contribution workflow.
42

## Approved Plan:
# Plan: Migrate SKF `data/` to `references/` + `assets/`

## Context

SKF defines the agentskills.io spec which specifies `references/` (detailed reference material) and `assets/` (templates, schemas, configs) as the standard subdirectories for skills. However, SKF's own 12 workflow skills use a non-spec `data/` directory as a catch-all for both types. This undermines spec credibility — the forge should exemplify its own standard.

## Approach

Split each skill's `data/` directory into `references/` and/or `assets/` based on file purpose:

- **`assets/`** = Output templates, format specs, schemas (structural files consumed to shape output)
- **`references/`** = Rules, patterns, protocols, heuristics (decision-making material loaded during execution)

Then update all path references in step files, knowledge files, and README.

## Classification

### Files → `assets/`  (14 files across 8 skills)

| Skill | File | Rationale |
|-------|------|-----------|
| skf-quick-skill | skill-template.md | Output template |
| skf-audit-skill | drift-report-template.md | Report template |
| skf-brief-skill | scope-templates.md | Scope presentation templates |
| skf-brief-skill | skill-brief-schema.md | Output schema |
| skf-create-skill | compile-assembly-rules.md | Output assembly format rules |
| skf-create-skill | skill-sections.md | SKILL.md output structure |
| skf-create-stack-skill | stack-skill-template.md | Output template |
| skf-export-skill | managed-section-format.md | Output format spec |
| skf-export-skill | snippet-format.md | Output format spec |
| skf-test-skill | output-section-formats.md | Report section formats |
| skf-verify-stack | feasibility-report-template.md | Report template |
| skf-analyze-source | skill-brief-schema.md | Output schema |

### Files → `references/`  (25 files across 11 skills)

| Skill | File | Rationale |
|-------|------|-----------|
| skf-quick-skill | registry-resolution.md | Resolution protocol |
| skf-audit-skill | severity-rules.md | Classification rules |
| skf-refine-architecture | refinement-rules.md | Analysis rules |
| skf-create-skill | extraction-patterns.md | Extraction heuristics |
| skf-create-skill | extraction-patterns-tracing.md | Tracing protocols |
| skf-create-skill | source-resolution-protocols.md | Resolution logic |
| skf-create-skill | tier-degradation-rules.md | Fallback rules |
| skf-create-stack-skill | compose-mode-rules.md | Composition rules |
| skf-create-stack-skill | integration-patterns.md | Detection patterns |
| skf-create-stack-skill | manifest-patterns.md | Manifest parsing rules |
| skf-setup-forge | tier-rules.md | Tier calculation rules |
| skf-test-skill | scoring-rules.md | Scoring algorithms |
| skf-test-skill | source-access-protocol.md | API resolution logic |
| skf-update-skill | manual-section-rules.md | Section detection rules |
| skf-update-skill | merge-conflict-rules.md | Merge strategies |
| skf-update-skill | remote-source-resolution.md | Clone protocols |
| skf-verify-stack | coverage-patterns.md | Detection rules |
| skf-verify-stack | integration-verification-rules.md | Cross-ref protocols |
| skf-analyze-source | unit-detection-heuristics.md | Detection heuristics |

## Execution Sequence

### Phase 1: Move files (12 skills)

For each skill with a `data/` directory:
1. Create `references/` and/or `assets/` as needed
2. Move files per classification above
3. Remove empty `data/` directory

Skills getting **both** directories: skf-quick-skill, skf-audit-skill, skf-brief-skill, skf-create-skill, skf-create-stack-skill, skf-export-skill, skf-test-skill, skf-verify-stack, skf-analyze-source

Skills getting **only `references/`**: skf-refine-architecture, skf-setup-forge, skf-update-skill

### Phase 2: Update path references in step files (~75 references across ~31 files)

All `../data/{file}` references become either `../references/{file}` or `../assets/{file}` depending on classification.

Cross-skill references (e.g., `../../skf-export-skill/data/managed-section-format.md` in skf-drop-skill and skf-rename-skill) update similarly.

### Phase 3: Update knowledge files (5 references)

- `knowledge/overview.md` — lines 5, 9, 46, 50: update `data/` mentions to reflect new structure
- `knowledge/agentskills-spec.md` — line 43: `create-skill/data/` → `create-skill/assets/`
- `knowledge/provenance-tracking.md` — line 128: `audit-skill/data/` → `audit-skill/references/`
- `knowledge/split-body-strategy.md` — line 53: `test-skill/data/` → `test-skill/references/`
- `knowledge/skf-knowledge-index.csv` — line 2: update description

### Phase 4: Update README

- `src/README.md` — update layout diagram and descriptions to reflect `references/` + `assets/` instead of `data/`

## Verification

1. `grep -r 'data/' src/ --include='*.md'` — should return zero hits for `data/` as a skill subdirectory path (only `forge-data/` context refs should remain)
2. Confirm no `data/` directories remain: `find src/ -name data -type d`
3. Confirm all moved files exist at new paths
4. Spot-check 2-3 step files to verify relative paths resolve correctly

[result-id: r15]

```
1	# SKF Knowledge Overview
2	
3	## Principle
4	
5	Agent-level knowledge files capture cross-cutting principles and patterns that Ferris applies across multiple workflows. They are distinct from workflow `data/` files, which contain step-specific operational artifacts. Knowledge is loaded just-in-time when a workflow step directs — never preloaded into context.
6	
7	## Rationale
8	
9	The SKF module has 14 workflows containing numerous operational `data/` files embedded in step directories. These data files define schemas, templates, scoring formulas, and extraction rules tightly coupled to individual steps. But several principles — zero hallucination, confidence tiers, provenance — cut across multiple workflows and need a single authoritative source rather than fragmented repetition.
10	
11	Without a knowledge folder:
12	- Cross-cutting principles get duplicated across workflow steps
13	- Updates to foundational rules require changes in multiple locations
14	- Ferris has no centralized reference for judgment calls that span workflows
15	
16	With knowledge files:
17	- Each principle has one canonical definition
18	- Workflow steps reference knowledge files instead of re-stating rules
19	- Ferris loads only the knowledge relevant to the current task
20	
21	## Knowledge Map
22	
23	| File                                                       | Purpose                                                           | Key Workflows                      |
24	|------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------|
25	| [zero-hallucination.md](zero-hallucination.md)             | Every claim traces to source; uncitable content excluded          | AN, BS, CS, QS, SS, US, AS, TS, RA |
26	| [confidence-tiers.md](confidence-tiers.md)                 | T1/T1-low/T2/T3 trust model and citation formats                  | AN, BS, CS, QS, SS, US, AS, TS, EX, RA, VS |
27	| [progressive-capability.md](progressive-capability.md)     | Quick/Forge/Forge+/Deep tier philosophy and behavior adaptation          | SF, AN, BS, CS, QS, SS, US, AS, TS, EX, VS, RA |
28	| [agentskills-spec.md](agentskills-spec.md)                 | agentskills.io output format principles and compliance            | CS, QS, SS, US, TS, EX             |
29	| [skill-lifecycle.md](skill-lifecycle.md)                   | End-to-end pipeline, artifact flow, workflow selection            | All 12                             |
30	| [provenance-tracking.md](provenance-tracking.md)           | Provenance-map.json entries and file_entries, evidence reports, claim traceability | CS, QS, SS, US, AS, TS             |
31	| [manual-section-integrity.md](manual-section-integrity.md) | \[MANUAL\] marker preservation and merge algorithm                | CS, US, AS, TS, EX                 |
32	| [qmd-registry.md](qmd-registry.md)                         | Progressive QMD collection registry and collection gate principle | SF, BS, CS, US, AS                 |
33	| [ccc-bridge.md](ccc-bridge.md)                             | ccc_bridge interface, indexing lifecycle, Forge+ discovery-before-extraction | SF, AN, CS, SS, US, AS             |
34	| [tool-resolution.md](tool-resolution.md)                   | Bridge-to-tool and subprocess-to-tool mapping per IDE environment | CS, QS, SS, US, AS, AN, BS, RA     |
35	| [architecture-verification.md](architecture-verification.md) | Pre-code verification pipeline, integration verdicts, iteration loop | VS, RA, SS                        |
36	| [doc-fetcher.md](doc-fetcher.md)                           | T3 external documentation fetching, quarantine rules, tool-agnostic | CS, QS                             |
37	| [split-body-strategy.md](split-body-strategy.md)           | Selective vs full split-body, detection pattern, agent accuracy   | CS, QS, US, TS, EX                |
38	| [version-paths.md](version-paths.md)                       | Version-aware storage layout, path templates, export manifest v2, migration | CS, QS, SS, US, AS, TS, EX, VS, RA |
39	| [overview.md](overview.md)                                 | Knowledge map and JiT loading protocol (this file)               | All (entry point)                  |
40	
41	## JiT Loading Protocol
42	
43	1. Ferris receives a task or workflow activation
44	2. Consult `skf-knowledge-index.csv` to identify relevant knowledge fragments by tags or description. The `tier` column classifies fragments as `core` (always relevant when the domain applies) or `extended` (load only when the specific scenario is active — e.g., `doc-fetcher.md` only when `doc_urls` are present in the brief)
45	3. Load only the files needed for the current task — never bulk-load all knowledge
46	4. Apply knowledge principles during workflow execution alongside step-specific `data/` files
47	
48	## Knowledge vs. Data
49	
50	| Aspect    | Knowledge Files (`knowledge/`)       | Data Files (`data/`)                    |
51	|-----------|--------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|
52	| Scope     | Cross-cutting, multi-workflow        | Single workflow, single step            |
53	| Content   | Principles, patterns, judgment rules | Schemas, templates, scoring formulas    |
54	| Loading   | JiT via index lookup                 | JiT via step instruction                |
55	| Examples  | Zero hallucination, confidence tiers | skill-brief-schema.md, scoring-rules.md |
56	| Stability | Rarely changes                       | Changes with workflow evolution         |
57	
58	## Related Fragments
59	
60	- [confidence-tiers.md](confidence-tiers.md) — the trust model that underpins extraction and scoring
61	- [skill-lifecycle.md](skill-lifecycle.md) — the end-to-end pipeline connecting all workflows
62	- [progressive-capability.md](progressive-capability.md) — tier philosophy that affects every workflow
63	
64	_Source: synthesized from module.yaml, README.md, and workflow README_
65
